in_dependencies): MultiCite citation\n  function typology (Lauscher, Ko, Kuehl, Johnson, Cohan, Jurgens & Lo, NAACL 2022)\n  reduced to 6 plain-English types: background / motivation / uses / extends /\n  similarities / differences (FUTURE_WORK dropped — every artifact in the trace\n  is already realized).\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom typing import Annotated, Literal\n\nfrom aii_lib.prompts import LLMPrompt, LLMPromptModel, LLMStructOut, LLMStructOutModel\nfrom pydantic import Field\n\n\nclass ArtifactRelation(LLMPromptModel, LLMStructOutModel):\n    \"\"\"One typed A↔A edge between a dependent artifact and one of its in_dependencies.\n\n    MultiCite citation-function typology (Lauscher et al., NAACL 2022),\n    reduced to 6 plain-English types.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    from_id: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=\"ID of the predecessor artifact (the one being depended on)\"\n    )\n    to_id: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=\"ID of the dependent artifact (the new artifact this iteration)\"\n    )\n    relation_type: Annotated[\n        Literal[\"background\", \"motivation\", \"uses\", \"extends\", \"similarities\", \"differences\"],\n        LLMPrompt,\n        LLMStructOut,\n    ] = Field(\n        description=(\n            \"MultiCite citation-function type for the predecessor→dependent edge: \"\n            \"'background' — predecessor is treated as background context; \"\n            \"'motivation' — predecessor motivated this artifact's research; \"\n            \"'uses' — this artifact uses the predecessor's data, method, or output; \"\n            \"'extends' — this artifact extends the predecessor; \"\n            \"'similarities' — this artifact's results agree with the predecessor's; \"\n            \"'differences' — this artifact's results disagree with the predecessor's.\"\n        )\n    )\n    relation_rationale: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        max_length=120,\n        description=\"Brief rationale for this relation type (one short line, max 120 characters).\",\n    )\n\n\nclass RevisedHypothesis(LLMPromptModel, LLMStructOutModel):\n    \"\"\"Revised hypothesis after reviewing iteration results.\n\n    Output matches the hypothesis dict structure so it can replace the\n    original hypothesis in subsequent iterations.\n\n    ``evidence_state`` / ``move`` / ``coverage`` / ``candidates_considered``\n    carry the between-iteration steering decision — the only one a run makes.\n    An audit of 19 finished runs found the narrow-salvage move taken ~20\n    times after a weak or null result and the widen move taken zero times,\n    with nothing in the output recording which move had been made, so the\n    bias was invisible in the run record as well as unconstrained in the\n    prompt. These fields make the decision explicit and checkable;\n    ``relation_type`` is kept only so runs already on disk still parse.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    kind: Literal[\"revised_hypothesis\"] = \"revised_hypothesis\"\n    title: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=\"Revised hypothesis title in plain, everyday language — short and jargon-free so a non-expert grasps it at a glance and it fits the run visualizations. Aim for about 4-8 words (~40 characters); may be unchanged if still accurate.\"\n    )\n    hypothesis: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=\"Revised hypothesis statement — what we now believe based on evidence\"\n    )\n    relation_rationale: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        max_length=120,\n        description=\"Brief rationale for the H↔H revision type (one short line, max 120 characters).\",\n    )\n    confidence_delta: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=\"How confidence changed: 'increased', 'decreased', or 'unchanged'\"\n    )\n    key_changes: Annotated[list[str], LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=\"Bullet list of specific changes made to the hypothesis\"\n    )\n    evidence_state: Annotated[\n        Literal[\"strong_survivor\", \"weak_or_null\", \"experiment_broken\"],\n        LLMPrompt,\n        LLMStructOut,\n    ] = Field(\n        description=(\n            \"What this iteration's evidence actually is, classified BEFORE any move is \"\n            \"chosen: 'strong_survivor' — an executed artifact supports the claim at a size \"\n            \"the original ask would recognise, and it survived the obvious alternative \"\n            \"explanations; 'weak_or_null' — the test ran and gave nothing to build on (no \"\n            \"effect, an effect indistinguishable from baseline, or one far smaller than the \"\n            \"ask implied); 'experiment_broken' — the test never tested the claim (defect in \"\n            \"code, data, measure, sample or setup, or numbers that were never executed), so \"\n            \"the claim is untested rather than refuted.\"\n        )\n    )\n    move: Annotated[\n        Literal[\"deepen\", \"extend\", \"widen\", \"fix\", \"declare\"], LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut\n    ] = Field(\n        description=(\n            \"The steering move for the NEXT iteration, read off evidence_state and the \"\n            \"remaining budget: 'deepen' — same claim, go after the mechanism or the boundary; \"\n            \"'extend' — same claim, new population/period/setting; 'widen' — return to the \"\n            \"user's original ask and put a population of alternative candidate answers in \"\n            \"play, screened cheaply and confirmed on held-out evidence; 'fix' — the claim is \"\n            \"unchanged and the defective test is repaired; 'declare' — write the run up as a \"\n            \"negative result, permitted ONLY on the final iteration or with no budget left.\"\n        )\n    )\n    move_rationale: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        max_length=200,\n        description=(\n            \"Why this move follows from this evidence_state and the remaining budget \"\n            \"(one short line, max 200 characters). For 'fix', name the defect.\"\n        ),\n    )\n    coverage: Annotated[Literal[\"full\", \"partial\", \"lost\"], LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=(\n            \"Does the revised hypothesis still answer the USER'S ORIGINAL ask? 'full' — it \"\n            \"answers the ask; 'partial' — it answers a recognisable piece of it; 'lost' — the \"\n            \"run has drifted onto a different question and the next iteration must go back.\"\n        )\n    )\n    coverage_statement: Annotated[str, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=(\n            \"One sentence naming which part of the user's original ask the next iteration \"\n            \"will answer.\"\n        )\n    )\n    candidates_considered: Annotated[int, LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        description=(\n            \"How many alternative claims, mechanisms, measures or bodies of evidence you \"\n            \"actually weighed during THIS revision. 1 when none were weighed — which, after \"\n            \"a weak_or_null result with budget remaining, means the widen was not done.\"\n        )\n    )\n    relation_type: Annotated[\n        Literal[\"evolution\", \"embedding\", \"replacement\"], LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut\n    ] = Field(\n        default=\"evolution\",\n        description=(\n            \"LEGACY, kept for backward compatibility with runs already on disk — 'move' is \"\n            \"the field that steers the run. Moulines's structuralist typology of this \"\n            \"revision: 'evolution' — refining specialised claims while keeping the same \"\n            \"conceptual frame; 'embedding' — the previous hypothesis is now a special case \"\n            \"of a broader frame; 'replacement' — rejecting the previous frame entirely.\"\n        ),\n    )\n    artifact_relations: Annotated[list[ArtifactRelation], LLMPrompt, LLMStructOut] = Field(\n        default_factory=list,\n        description=(\n            \"Typed A↔A edges for this iteration's new artifacts. Emit one entry \"\n            \"per (predecessor → dependent) edge for every in_dependency on each \"\n            \"artifact produced this iteration.\"\n        ),\n    )\n\n    def to_hypothesis_dict(self, original: dict) -> dict:\n        \"\"\"Merge revised fields into the original hypothesis dict.\n\n        Preserves metadata fields (hypothesis_id, is_seeded, model, seeds)\n        while updating content fields.\n        \"\"\"\n        updated = dict(original)\n        updated[\"title\"] = self.title\n        updated[\"hypothesis\"] = self.hypothesis\n        updated[\"_relation_rationale\"] = self.relation_rationale\n        updated[\"_confidence_delta\"] = self.confidence_delta\n        updated[\"_key_changes\"] = self.key_changes\n        updated[\"_evidence_state\"] = self.evidence_state\n        updated[\"_move\"] = self.move\n        updated[\"_move_rationale\"] = self.move_rationale\n        updated[\"_coverage\"] = self.coverage\n        updated[\"_coverage_statement\"] = self.coverage_statement\n        updated[\"_candidates_considered\"] = self.candidates_considered\n        updated[\"relation_type\"] = self.relation_type\n        return updated\n=====SPROMPT\n\"\"\"System prompt for upd_hypo (Step 3.6: UPD_HYPO).\n\nInternal hypothesis revision — \"Where does the next iteration point?\"\nSame LLM as the pipeline (no adversarial intent, just honest revision).\n\nThis is the run's ONLY between-iteration steering decision, so the framing\nhere is the classify-then-move procedure from\n``components/evidence_moves.py`` rather than the old stability framing — see\nthat module for the 19-run audit that replaced it.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom aii_pipeline.prompts.components.aii_context import get_aii_context\n\n\ndef PROMPT(context: str) -> str:\n    return f\"\"\"{context}\n\nYou are deciding where a research run points next, using the evidence it\ngathered this iteration. Your revised hypothesis IS the next iteration's\nhypothesis — nothing else steers the run — so this is a steering decision\nfirst and a piece of honest reflection second.\n\nSCOPE: Your ONLY output is the revised hypothesis text. You do NOT run code,\nproduce artifacts, fix bugs, or otherwise act on the evidence yourself — the\nnext iteration of the invention loop will spawn fresh artifacts based on your\nrevised hypothesis. Reflect on the evidence and rewrite the hypothesis;\nnothing else.\n\nPRINCIPLES:\n- Ground every revision in specific artifacts and results. A number that was\n  projected, assumed or left as a placeholder is not a result.\n- CLASSIFY THE EVIDENCE BEFORE CHOOSING A MOVE. The move follows from the\n  classification and the remaining budget by a fixed rule; it is not a\n  free judgement, because that is exactly where past runs went wrong.\n- A weak or null result with budget left is a signal to go WIDER, not\n  smaller. The question the user asked is still open; the answer you tried\n  is the only thing that was refuted.\n- Never shrink the claim until the effect you happened to observe becomes\n  the claim. A finding nobody needed is worse than an honest negative.\n- A clean negative result is a LAST RESORT, not a deliverable. It is the\n  right output only when no iteration and no candidate remain.\n- Increase specificity as evidence accumulates; do not inflate confidence\n  without strong evidence.\n- Revise hypothesis text only — never attempt to address feedback by running\n  code, proposing fixes, or producing artifacts; the next loop iteration\n  handles all artifact generation.\n\"\"\"\n\n\ndef get() -> str:\n    \"\"\"Get system prompt for hypothesis revision.\"\"\"\n    return PROMPT(context=get_aii_context(focus=\"upd_hypo\"))", "stderr": "\nShell cwd was reset to /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_fcYd_7ruOwtm/3_invention_loop/iter_5/upd_hypo/upd_hypo", "interrupted": false, "isImage": false, "noOutputExpected": false}

{"stdout": "\"\"\"The between-iteration steering rule: classify the evidence, then move.\n\n``upd_hypo`` is the ONLY place a run decides where iteration N+1 points. An\naudit of 19 finished runs across several fields (linguistics, machine\nlearning, an ecology-to-ML transfer, and others) read every one of the ~40\nrevision decisions those runs made. After a weak or null first result the\nrevision NARROWED onto a smaller version of the same claim about 20 times,\ndeclared a null 6 times, and widened — went back to the user's original\nquestion and put more than one candidate answer in play — ZERO times.\n\nThe outcome split on exactly that. Every run that finished with a positive,\nnon-obvious result either got a strong effect on its first test or had more\nthan one candidate alive at once (several sub-questions, several variants, a\nthird comparison added mid-run). Every run that started from a single\nhypothesis and got a weak first result narrowed onto a tiny effect in the\ndirection everyone already expected and shipped that as the finding. The one\nhuman-steered success did the opposite after its refutation: back to the\noriginal question, ~50 candidates generated, a cheap screen over all of them,\nthe winner picked on held-out data, and only then depth.\n\nNothing in the prompts caused the narrowing. The old revision prompt said\n\"STABILITY IS OK\", \"keep the core idea\", \"preserve the core AII prompt\" and\n\"treat null results as valuable contributions\" — four separate instructions\nall pointing the same way, with no instruction pointing the other way and no\nname for the situation in which the other way is right. Narrow-salvage was\nthe only move the prompt described, so it was the move that got made.\n\nSo the fix is not encouragement. It is a two-step procedure with a lookup\ntable: name the evidence state, then read the move off the state and the\nremaining budget. The model still judges which state it is in — that\njudgement is real and cannot be mechanized from here — but it no longer gets\nto choose the move once it has, because that choice is where the bias lived.\n\nFIELD-NEUTRAL BY CONSTRUCTION. A run's question can be about syntax, markets,\nspecies, elections, proof techniques or model families, so every word below\nis chosen to hold in any of them: \"candidate\" rather than \"model variant\",\n\"measure\" rather than \"metric\", \"cases\" rather than \"datasets\". The audit\nhappened to cover mostly ML runs; wording that reads as ML-only would quietly\nteach a linguistics run that the rule is not addressed to it.\n\nShared by ``upd_hypo`` (which picks the move) and ``gen_strat`` (which has to\nbuild the iteration that move implies) so the two cannot drift apart — a\nwiden decision that the next strategy step turns into one more deep test is\nthe same failure with an extra step in it.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\n#: The evidence states, in the order the classification step presents them.\n#: Pinned here because ``upd_hypo``'s schema declares the same literals and a\n#: prompt naming a state the schema rejects is an unparseable answer.\nEVIDENCE_STATES: tuple[str, ...] = (\n    \"strong_survivor\",\n    \"weak_or_null\",\n    \"experiment_broken\",\n)\n\n#: The moves, likewise mirrored by the schema's ``move`` literal.\nMOVES: tuple[str, ...] = (\"deepen\", \"extend\", \"widen\", \"fix\", \"declare\")\n\n#: How much of the user's ORIGINAL ask the current hypothesis still answers.\n#: Carried by both the reviser and the paper reviewer so a run cannot drift\n#: off the question it was asked without one of them saying so.\nCOVERAGE_STATES: tuple[str, ...] = (\"full\", \"partial\", \"lost\")\n\n\n_GUARDRAIL = \"\"\"<screening_discipline>\nWidening multiplies the number of claims in play, and a population of\ncandidates screened on one body of evidence will always contain one that\nlooks good by chance. So a widen is only honest with the discipline attached:\n\n- Report `candidates_considered` — how many alternative claims you actually\n  weighed this revision, not how many you could imagine. 1 means you weighed\n  none, and after a weak or null result with budget left, 1 is a failure to\n  do the move.\n- A candidate is SCREENED on one body of evidence and CONFIRMED on another\n  that the screen never touched — a held-out split, a later period, a\n  different population, corpus, site, cohort or case set. Say in the revised\n  hypothesis which evidence is which.\n- The winner of a screen is a CANDIDATE, never yet a finding. Do not write a\n  screening result as the answer, and do not report the best of several\n  screened effects as though it had been the only one tested.\n- Never re-screen on the confirmation evidence after seeing it. If the\n  confirmation fails, that candidate is dead; go back to the population, do\n  not go hunting for a subgroup where it survives.\n</screening_discipline>\"\"\"\n\n\n_NARROW_BAN = \"\"\"<narrow_salvage_ban>\nThe failure this procedure exists to stop: a weak or null first result, and\nthe revision quietly shrinks the claim until whatever effect the data did\nshow becomes the claim — a smaller population, a milder verb, a subgroup, a\nweaker measure, an effect in the direction everyone already expected. Each\nstep is defensible. The run ends with a finding nobody needed.\n\nSo: you may NOT narrow the claim onto an effect that is small relative to\nwhat the original ask implied, UNLESS the paper can state why that small\neffect is ITSELF the answer to the ask — a bound someone needed, a mechanism\nthat only shows up at that size, a belief it overturns. If you cannot write\nthat sentence, the move is `widen`, not a smaller claim.\n</narrow_salvage_ban>\"\"\"\n\n\ndef get_evidence_move_procedure(*, iteration: int, max_iterations: int) -> str:\n    \"\"\"The classify-then-move decision procedure, budget-aware.\n\n    ``iteration`` / ``max_iterations`` are rendered into the text rather than\n    left for the model to infer, because the whole table turns on whether\n    there is another iteration to spend: ``declare`` is correct on the last\n    one and a premature surrender on any other.\n    \"\"\"\n    remaining = max(max_iterations - iteration, 0)\n    budget_line = (\n        f\"There are {remaining} iteration(s) AFTER this one.\"\n        if remaining\n        else \"This is the FINAL iteration — no iteration follows this one.\"\n    )\n    return f\"\"\"<evidence_state_and_move>\nThis is iteration {iteration} of {max_iterations}. {budget_line}\nYour revision is the ONLY thing that decides where the next iteration points,\nso work the two steps below in order and do not skip to a conclusion.\n\nSTEP 1 — CLASSIFY THE EVIDENCE. Set `evidence_state` to exactly one of:\n\n- \"strong_survivor\": an artifact that ACTUALLY RAN produced a result that\n  supports the claim, at a size the original ask would recognise as an\n  answer, and it survived the obvious alternative explanations — the\n  baseline, the confound, the simpler account.\n- \"weak_or_null\": the test ran and gave you nothing to build on. No effect,\n  an effect you cannot distinguish from the baseline or from noise, or an\n  effect so much smaller than the ask implied that reporting it would answer\n  a different question than the one asked.\n- \"experiment_broken\": the test never tested the claim. A defect in the code,\n  the data, the measure, the sample or the setup — including a run that did\n  not finish, or numbers that were projected rather than executed. The claim\n  is UNTESTED here, not refuted.\n\nSTEP 2 — READ THE MOVE OFF THE STATE. Set `move` by this rule. It is a\nlookup, not a judgement — the judgement was STEP 1:\n\n- \"strong_survivor\" -> `deepen` (why does it hold — the mechanism, the\n  boundary where it stops) or `extend` (where else does it hold — a new\n  population, period, language, species, market, model family, case set).\n- \"weak_or_null\" AND at least one iteration remains -> `widen`. MANDATORY.\n  Not \"consider widening\". This is the decision the audit found being missed\n  every single time.\n- \"experiment_broken\" -> `fix`. Keep the claim EXACTLY as it is, name the\n  defect precisely in `move_rationale`, and say what a correct test looks\n  like. Do not reframe, soften or re-scope a claim that was never tested —\n  that hides the defect behind a new hypothesis.\n- `declare` (write the run up as a negative result) ONLY when this is the\n  final iteration, or no budget remains to test anything further. A clean\n  null is a last resort, not a deliverable, and it is never the right move\n  while an untried candidate and an iteration both exist.\n\nHOW TO WIDEN, when the rule says widen:\n\n1. Go back to the USER'S ORIGINAL ASK — not to the hypothesis you just\n   refuted. The refuted hypothesis was one answer to that ask; the ask is\n   still open.\n2. Enumerate a POPULATION of candidate answers to it — alternative claims,\n   alternative mechanisms that would produce the observed non-result,\n   alternative measures of the same thing, alternative bodies of evidence,\n   alternative comparisons. Aim for many and cheap, not one and careful.\n   Write down how many you weighed.\n3. Propose a CHEAP SCREEN that tests all of them at once, coarsely, at a cost\n   comparable to one deep test — and a HELD-OUT CONFIRMATION that the screen\n   never saw, for whichever candidate survives it.\n4. The revised hypothesis is then EITHER the single best surviving candidate,\n   stated as a claim, OR — if the screen still has to be run — an explicit\n   SCREENING hypothesis that names the population and the selection rule.\n   Both are legitimate outputs of a widen; a restatement of the old claim is\n   not.\n\n{_NARROW_BAN}\n\n{_GUARDRAIL}\n\nCOVERAGE. Independently of the move, answer: does the hypothesis you are\nabout to write still answer the USER'S ORIGINAL ASK? Set `coverage` to\n\"full\" (it answers the ask), \"partial\" (it answers a recognisable piece of\nit) or \"lost\" (the run has drifted onto a different question), and write one\nsentence in `coverage_statement` saying which part of the ask the next\niteration will answer. \"lost\" is not a failure to hide — it is the signal\nthat the next iteration must go back to the ask.\n</evidence_state_and_move>\"\"\"\n\n\n#: gen_strat's half of the contract. The move is decided in ``upd_hypo``, but\n#: it is ``gen_strat`` that spends the iteration, so a widen has to change the\n#: SHAPE of the strategy — several cheap candidate tests running in parallel\n#: plus a held-out selection — rather than producing one more deep test with a\n#: wider title. The two texts live in one module so neither can be edited\n#: without the other being read.\nWIDE_SCREEN_BLOCK = \"\"\"<wide_screen_iteration>\nTHIS ITERATION IS A WIDE SCREEN, NOT A DEEP TEST.\n\nYou are here because the previous iteration's evidence was weak or null and\nthe revision widened (`_move` is \"widen\" on the hypothesis), or because the\nrequest is open-ended, this is the first iteration, and the hypothesis\ncarries alternates that answer the same ask by different mechanisms. Either\nway the bottleneck is not depth on one candidate — it is that only one\ncandidate has ever been in play.\n\nSo build the iteration like this:\n\n- Spend the artifact budget on SEVERAL candidates tested in parallel, each\n  cheaply and coarsely, rather than on one candidate tested thoroughly. Three\n  to six candidates at a third of the depth beats one at full depth here.\n- Screen every candidate on the SAME evidence, with the SAME measure, so the\n  comparison between them is real.\n- Reserve evidence the screen never touches — a held-out split, a later\n  period, a different population, corpus, site, cohort or case set — and say\n  in `expected_outcome` that the surviving candidate gets confirmed there\n  before anything is claimed.\n- State the selection rule BEFORE the screen runs: which measure decides,\n  and what margin counts as surviving. Its output is a ranked, honestly-reported comparison plus one\nconfirmed survivor — not a finished finding.\n</wide_screen_iteration>\"\"\"\n\n\n__all__ = [\n    \"COVERAGE_STATES\",\n    \"EVIDENCE_STATES\",\n    \"MOVES\",\n    \"WIDE_SCREEN_BLOCK\",\n    \"get_evidence_move_procedure\",\n]\n=====ORIG\n706:<prompt>\n15537:{\u001b[38;5;81m\"stdout\"\u001b[0m\u001b[38;5;28m: \" 4353 out_schema.py\\n 4462 s_prompt.py\\n 6860 u_prompt.py\\n15675 total\\n=== S ===\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"System prompt for review_hypo — adversarial hypothesis review before invention loop.\\n\\nReview only (no revision). Uses web search to ground review in evidence.\\nScoring scales and review principles come from the shared review_rubric\\ncomponent; hypothesis-specific phrasing is passed as parameters.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nfrom aii_pipeline.prompts.components.aii_context import get_aii_context\\nfrom aii_pipeline.prompts.components.ambition import AMBITION_REVIEW_PRINCIPLES\\nfrom aii_pipeline.prompts.components.review_rubric import get_review_rubric\\nfrom aii_pipeline.prompts.components.tool_calling import get_web_tool_guidance\\n\\n_ASSESSMENT_DIMENSIONS = \\\"\\\"\\\"(a) Originality: Are the ideas new? Search the\\\"\\n            \\\" web for the proposed idea, its method name, and its central claim. If the\\\"\\n            \\\" idea already exists, say so and name the source — this is the cheapest point\\\"\\n            \\\" in the pipeline to catch it\\\",\\n            \\\"- Distinguish a genuinely new idea from a restatement of known work in new\\\"\\n            \\\" vocabulary. Coining a term for an existing method is not originality, and\\\"\\n            \\\" should be scored as a major issue\\\",\\n            *AMBITION_REVIEW_PRINCIPLES,\\n        ),\\n    )\\n    return f\\\"\\\"\\\"{context}\\n\\nROLE: You are a very experienced and critical conference reviewer.\\nYour expertise spans the domain of the hypothesis under review.\\nYou have served on program committees at top-tier venues in the relevant field.\\n\\nTASK: Perform a deep and honest review (at the level of a top-tier venue submission) of\\nthis research hypothesis BEFORE any experiments have been run.\\n\\nGOAL: Your review feeds directly back to the hypothesis author. {"stdout": "\\title{One Candidate Survives: A Pre-Registered Screen of Internal Safety Metrics for Language Model Checkpoints}\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nOpen-weight language models ship faster than anyone can audit them. A metric computable from a single checkpoint's weights or activations, without generating text or consulting an external judge, could cut screening costs by orders of magnitude. We pre-register a broad panel of such candidates and evaluate most of them across several chat model families against two-sided ground truth that penalises both harmful compliance and false refusal. Under six pre-registered selection rules frozen in advance, exactly one candidate passes: twin d-prime (C12), a cross-fitted measure of how well a model's mid-layer activations separate harmful from safe prompts, with Spearman $\\rho = 0.61$ against balanced safety. The strongest raw correlation instead belongs to self-ablation sensitivity (C2, $\\rho = 0.90$), but its surviving variants turn out to be monotone transforms of one underlying measurement, and neither metric adds significant predictive power beyond simply generating and judging a small set of completions. We also compare against the published AMS incumbent, which loses to both C2 and a simple first-token logit gap on this panel, and we document a right-padding bug in its reference implementation.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nPublic model registries now host hundreds of thousands of open-weight language model checkpoints. Many are derivatives of a few base models, produced by safety fine-tuning, preference optimisation, abliteration or direct weight editing. A registry operator or downstream deployer faces a screening problem: which checkpoints are safe to serve, and which have had their safety training degraded or removed?\n\nA full behavioural evaluation requires generating completions to a prompt suite and scoring them with a rubric. This scales linearly with the number of checkpoints. If a metric computable from the model's own weights or hidden states, on at most a handful of prompts, could rank checkpoints by safety, it would cut this cost by orders of magnitude. We call such a quantity a \\emph{cheap safety metric}: one computable from a single checkpoint without its parent model, using at most 16 forward passes and no text generation.\n\nPrior work has shown that safety-trained and abliterated models differ in their activation geometry. Refusal is mediated by a low-rank subspace \\cite{Arditi2024}, harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately \\cite{Zhao2025}, and linear probes can detect harmful intent in residual streams \\cite{LlorenteSaguer2026a, LlorenteSaguer2026b}. Several reference-free safety scores have been proposed, including AMS \\cite{Messenger2026}, RAS \\cite{Huang2026}, N-GLARE \\cite{Lin2025} and Skin-Deep \\cite{Lee2026}. However, these methods evaluate on one-sided ground truth: they measure whether a model refuses harmful prompts but ignore whether it complies with benign ones. A blanket refuser scores perfectly on harm-refusal rate but is useless.\n\nThe missing ingredient is \\emph{two-sided} ground truth. We define a balanced safety score $S_2 = \\tfrac{1}{2}(\\text{harm-refusal rate}) + \\tfrac{1}{2}(\\text{benign-compliance rate})$ on a 94-item set that includes both harmful prompts and safe look-alikes (Section~\\ref{sec:d2}). Under $S_2$, a blanket refuser scores 0.5, not 1.0. This makes the screening problem harder and more realistic: a metric must track genuine safety, not mere refusal frequency.\n\nWe organise the investigation as a pre-registered screen with six selection rules, frozen before any scoring. The screen covers 14 of the 16 registered candidates plus a comparator and the published AMS incumbent, evaluated on 23 graded checkpoints from 8 families. One candidate clears all six rules. Several others, including the highest-correlation read, fail the rules designed to distinguish genuine safety signal from artefacts of the measurement.\n\n[FIGURE:fig_overview]\n\n\\paragraph{Summary of contributions.}\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{The screen.} We pre-register 16 candidate metrics and execute 14 of them (C4 and C5 remain untested) on 23 graded checkpoints from 8 architecture families against balanced two-sided safety. Under the six pre-registered rules, one candidate passes: twin d-prime (C12, $\\rho = 0.61$ $[0.36, 0.87]$). Self-ablation sensitivity (C2, $\\rho = 0.90$) clears the repaired direction-specificity and pole-control gates, but its three surviving variants (C2, C2n, C17) are monotone transforms of the same measurement (Section~\\ref{sec:screen}).\n\\item \\textbf{Two-sided ground truth on CORE-94.} We score 23 checkpoints on 94 items (66 harmful, 28 XSTest-safe) with 64-token greedy decoding and a Gemini-2.5-Flash stance judge, constructing balanced and product two-sided targets. A secondary judge (GPT-5-mini) agrees at $\\kappa = 0.72$ on a pooled 25\\% seed (Section~\\ref{sec:d2}).\n\\item \\textbf{AMS incumbent comparison.} Every variant of the published AMS metric \\cite{Messenger2026} correlates worse than C2 with two-sided safety on this panel, with paired confidence intervals excluding zero. The reference implementation (ams-scanner 0.1.3) contains a right-padding bug that reads PAD-token activations (Section~\\ref{sec:ams}).\n\\item \\textbf{Content-free pull control.} Following Malla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026}, we subtract a norm-matched random steer from C1. The corrected metric (C1n) correlates at $\\rho = 0.76$, essentially unchanged from C1 ($\\rho(C1, C1n) = 0.995$), refuting the hypothesis that C1's signal is a content-free pull toward refusal (Section~\\ref{sec:malla}).\n\\item \\textbf{Grader-side refusal.} LLM judges under the StrongREJECT rubric \\cite{Souly2024} assign the degenerate score tuple $(1,1,1)$ to 65.9\\% of harmful items, with 328 vs.\\ 67 discordant items against a stance-based framing (Section~\\ref{sec:grader}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Activation geometry of safety.}\nArditi et al.\\ \\cite{Arditi2024} showed that refusal in chat models is mediated by a single direction in the residual stream. Zhao et al.\\ \\cite{Zhao2025} demonstrated that harmfulness and refusal occupy separate subspaces. Llorente-Saguer \\cite{LlorenteSaguer2026a, LlorenteSaguer2026b} fit harm-direction probes via angular deviation, and Lee et al.\\ \\cite{Lee2026} diagnosed alignment fragility through activation geometry. Joad et al.\\ \\cite{Joad2026} argued that refusal involves more than one direction, consistent with our finding that the self-ablation signal is concentrated in the safer half of the panel (Section~\\ref{sec:detect_grade}). These papers establish that safety leaves a geometric trace in activations but do not ask whether that trace predicts a two-sided safety score across diverse families.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-free safety scoring.}\nMessenger \\cite{Messenger2026} (AMS) reports Pearson $r = -0.55$ ($p = .04$, $n = 14$) between an activation-space statistic and JailbreakBench compliance, with 96 forward passes per model. Huang et al.\\ \\cite{Huang2026} (RAS) compute a refusal-alignment score requiring a family-specific reference model. Lin et al.\\ \\cite{Lin2025} (N-GLARE, ACL 2026) build a non-generative evaluator using 7{,}000+ probing cases. Li et al.\\ \\cite{Li2026} correlate steering-dose slopes with safety, and Leong et al.\\ \\cite{Leong2025} attribute safety to template-processing sites. Hurtado \\cite{Hurtado2026} audits abliteration across families, reporting AUROC 0.95 and leave-one-family-out balanced accuracy 0.89 on a detection task. None of these methods test against two-sided ground truth that penalises false refusal.\n\n\\paragraph{Content-free steering artefacts.}\nMalla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026} showed that random steering vectors pull small models toward refusal independently of the vector's semantic content, questioning whether activation-based safety reads measure safety or merely detect a model's susceptibility to perturbation. We test this directly by subtracting a norm-matched random steer from C1 (Section~\\ref{sec:malla}).\n\n\\paragraph{Safety evaluation methodology.}\nStrongREJECT \\cite{Souly2024} provides a rubric-based judge for jailbreak evaluation. XSTest \\cite{Rottger2023} pairs harmful prompts with safe look-alikes. OR-Bench \\cite{Cui2024} measures single-turn over-refusal. GuidedBench \\cite{Huang2025} and Mu et al.\\ \\cite{Mu2026} address judge-side refusal and evaluator agreement. Qi et al.\\ \\cite{Qi2024} showed that safety alignment can be shallow, affecting only the first few tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight editing and gameability.}\nROSI \\cite{AbuShairah2025} amplifies refusal by injecting a rank-one matrix into the o\\_proj and down\\_proj residual-write matrices. Tamirisa et al.\\ \\cite{Tamirisa2024} developed tamper-resistant safeguards. Muhamed et al.\\ \\cite{Muhamed2026} introduced decoy-direction optimisation, a post-hoc defence against abliteration that could also be used to game weight-based safety reads such as C2 and C5. Item-response-theory approaches to efficient safety scoring have been proposed by Rivera et al.\\ \\cite{Rivera2026} and Spagliardi et al.\\ \\cite{Spagliardi2026}.\n\n\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Two-Sided Ground Truth}\\label{sec:d2}\n\nEach checkpoint is scored on the CORE-94 item set: 66 harmful-side items (44 from JailbreakBench and StrongREJECT, 22 XSTest contrast items) and 28 XSTest-safe items (benign prompts phrased to resemble harmful ones). Completions are generated with greedy decoding at 64 new tokens and judged by Gemini-2.5-Flash using a stance-based framing that asks whether the model engaged with or declined the request. A secondary judge (GPT-5-mini) scores a pooled 25\\% random seed across the panel, achieving inter-judge $\\kappa = 0.72$ (agreement 86.3\\%, $n = 525$ items).\n\nThe \\emph{balanced} two-sided safety score is:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:s2}\nS_2 = \\tfrac{1}{2}\\,(\\text{harm-refusal rate on 66 harmful items}) + \\tfrac{1}{2}\\,(1 - \\text{false-refusal rate on 28 XSTest-safe items}).\n\\end{equation}\nA blanket refuser scores $S_2 = 0.5$. The \\emph{product} variant $P_2 = (\\text{harm-refusal}) \\times (1 - \\text{false-refusal})$ scores a blanket refuser at~0. Both are reported; $S_2$ is the primary target throughout.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-experiment settings. All experiments use the same 23 graded checkpoints. CORE-94 = 66 harmful + 28 XSTest-safe items. SCREEN16 = 8 harmful/benign-twin pairs (16 prompts). The ROSI side arm uses its own generation length.}\n\\label{tab:settings}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nExperiment & Ground truth & Gen.\\ tokens & Judge & Prompts & Device \\\\\n\\midrule\nPanel labels ($S_2$) & CORE-94 & 64 & Gemini-2.5-Flash & 94 & --- \\\\\nMetric screen & --- & --- & --- & 16 (SCREEN16) & GPU (bf16) \\\\\nAMS incumbent & --- & --- & --- & 16 / 96 & GPU (bf16) \\\\\nROSI side arm & 64 harm + 32 twin & 20 / 128 & GPT-5-mini / Gemini & 96 & GPU \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Panel}\\label{sec:panel}\n\nWe measure 36 checkpoints from 8 architecture families (Qwen3, Qwen2.5, TinyLlama, Llama-3.2, OLMo-2, SmolLM2, Falcon3, Danube3) spanning 11 lineages. All are at most 4B parameters and load in bf16. Of the 36, 23 are graded (have two-sided labels), 12 are ungraded (metric values computed; labels deferred), and 1 is a base model without chat formatting. Two graded checkpoints are blanket refusers (Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B CensorTune variants). Eleven checkpoints are honest instruct models (from families with $\\geq$3 graded members), used in the pole-control rule. Twelve blind Set~A checkpoints were frozen before any Set~A label existed; Phi-3.5-mini-instruct was skipped (exceeded the 10.5\\,GB VRAM budget), leaving 11 frozen blind.\n\nAt the panel's realised size ($n = 23$; intraclass correlation ICC $= 0.103$ across 11 lineages, i.e.\\ sibling checkpoints from the same base model are only slightly more alike than unrelated ones), the minimum detectable effect at 80\\% power is $|\\rho| = 0.53$. Null results at this panel size mean ``not detectable,'' not ``absent.''\n\n\\subsection{Candidate Metrics}\\label{sec:candidates}\n\nWe pre-register 16 candidates grouped into seven conceptual families (PREREG SHA-256: \\texttt{9da2b165}), each reading hidden states or weights of a single model on at most 16 prompts (the frozen SCREEN16 set, 8 harmful/benign-twin pairs drawn from XSTest and JailbreakBench):\n\n\\paragraph{I. Causal gain (C1--C3).} C1: finite-difference harm-to-refusal gain (perturb the residual stream by the harm direction at 50\\% depth, measure the refusal-probability change). C2: two-sided self-ablation sensitivity (ablate the harm direction, measure the drop in harmful-vs-benign separation relative to an anisotropy-matched random direction). C3: twin-patching flip depth (patch activations from a harmful twin into its benign twin, measure at which layer the output flips).\n\n\\paragraph{II. Direction-conditioned weight paths (C4--C5).} C4: writer capacity along the refusal axis. C5: MLP-path gain from harm to refusal directions. Both remain untested.\n\n\\paragraph{III. Component and routing (C6--C9).} C6: logit-attribution concentration (cross-fitted, in-sample and lens variants measured). C7: attention mass on twin-differing tokens. C8: gradient$\\times$input share on twin-differing tokens. C9: template-site share \\cite{Leong2025}.\n\n\\paragraph{IV. Depth and site (C10--C11).} C10: decodability-minus-drive area. C11: first 8-token response-window commitment trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{V. Two-sided geometry (C12--C13).} C12: twin d-prime along a cross-fitted harm axis at the middle layer (cross-fitted: the harm direction is estimated on one half of the 16 prompts and applied to the other). C13: presentation invariance across prompt rephrasings.\n\n\\paragraph{VI. Learned (C14).} A ridge metamodel combining all available features under leave-one-family-out cross-validation, with a 500-permutation identity-only control.\n\n\\paragraph{VII. Label-free (C15--C16).} C15: late effective rank (spectral dispersion at final layers). C16: steering-dose slope (comparator only, replication of Li et al.\\ \\cite{Li2026}).\n\nThe logit-only baselines enter as bars, never as survivors: the first-token logit gap (the difference between the model's logit for a refusal-indicating first token and a compliance-indicating first token) and refusal-token mass (the softmax probability mass on refusal tokens at that position). C4 and C5 were not executed and are listed as NOT RUN. We also tested C1n (C1 minus a norm-matched random steer, following Malla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026}), C2n (C2 standardised by its own direction null), C2ts (a two-stage variant of C2), and C17 (the mean reference-rank of C2 and the logit gap).\n\n\\subsection{Selection Rules}\\label{sec:rules}\n\nA candidate passes the screen if it satisfies all six pre-registered rules (hashed before any scoring):\n\n\\begin{description}\n\\item[S1 (incremental signal).] The one-sided 95\\% lineage-cluster bootstrap CI on the partial Spearman $\\rho$ with $S_2$, after removing the logit gap and $\\log_{10}$ parameter count, excludes zero.\n\\item[S2 (direction specificity).] (a) The within-lineage label-permutation $p < 0.05$, and (b) the metric exceeds its anisotropy-matched random-direction null at the 95th percentile on a majority of the graded panel.\n\\item[S3 (pole control).] The declared orientation holds, and both wrapped poles (always-refuse and never-refuse system prompts) plus blanket refusers score worse than the honest instruct checkpoint from the same family.\n\\item[S4 (sign agreement).] Same sign at checkpoint and family aggregation.\n\\item[S5 (efficiency).] At most 16 prompts and under 2 minutes per $\\sim$4B model.\n\\item[S6 (beyond confounds).] Beats the family-only and size-only out-of-fold predictors.\n\\end{description}\n\nS2(b) and S3 were repaired before the iteration-5 evaluation: the original S2(b) required $\\geq$60\\% of models to exceed the random-direction null, a threshold that only the in-sample AMS variant met (at 100\\%, consistent with overfitting). The repaired form requires a simple majority. The original S3 required all $2 \\times 11$ pole checks to hold; the repair requires all checks that are evaluable (excluding models whose candidate value is near zero, $|C| < 0.01$, where $C$ is the candidate's raw score and the pole comparison is noise at that scale). Both original and repaired verdicts are reported.\n\n\\section{Experimental Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nAll measurements run on a single NVIDIA L4 GPU (24\\,GB) in bf16 precision. VRAM peaks at 9.7\\,GB for 4B models. Per-model wall time ranges from 13 to 107 seconds (median 31). The 23 graded checkpoints span 360M to 4B parameters. The 16-prompt SCREEN16 set, the selection rules and the candidate list were frozen (SHA-256 hashed) before any metric value was computed. All 1{,}195 stance-judge lookups hit cache (\\$0.00 incremental cost). Set~A values were frozen (SHA-256: \\texttt{95d97ac3}) at 2026-09-21T23:21:43Z, before any Set~A label existed. Code, checkpoint values and null distributions are released.\n\n\\section{Results}\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{The Screen}\\label{sec:screen}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:screen} reports the selection-rule verdicts for all executed candidates. One candidate passes all six rules: C12 (twin d-prime), with $\\rho = 0.61$ $[0.36, 0.87]$ under the balanced target and partial $\\rho = 0.43$ (one-sided 5\\% lineage-bootstrap bound $0.006$, clearing S1 by a narrow margin). C12 is significant within Qwen3 ($\\rho = 0.67$, $n = 8$) and Qwen2.5 ($\\rho = 0.70$, $n = 5$) but not TinyLlama ($\\rho = -0.10$, $n = 5$).\n\n[FIGURE:fig_c12_scatter]\n\nUnder the repaired S2(b) and S3 rules, three additional candidates survive: C2 ($\\rho = 0.90$), C2n ($\\rho = 0.82$) and C17 ($\\rho = 0.87$). However, these three are \\emph{not independent measurements}: C2n is C2 standardised by its own direction null, and C17 is the mean reference-rank of C2 and the logit gap. All three are monotone functions of the same self-ablation measurement plus, for C17, the logit-gap bar. Shipping three survivors satisfies the letter of a ``three independent internal metrics'' requirement but not its spirit.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pre-registered screen on the balanced target ($n = 23$ graded checkpoints, 8 families, 11 lineages). $\\rho$: Spearman with $S_2$. Partial $\\rho$: Spearman after removing logit gap and $\\log_{10}$ size (for bars: after removing $\\log_{10}$ size only). CI: 95\\% lineage-cluster bootstrap. $\\checkmark$ = pass, $\\times$ = fail. Rules S2(b) and S3 are the repaired forms; original verdicts are noted where they differ.}\n\\label{tab:screen}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccccc}\n\\toprule\nCandidate & $\\rho$ [CI] & Partial (bound) & S1 & S2 & S3 & S4 & S5 & S6 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\emph{Survivors}} \\\\\nC12 (twin d-prime) & $.61$ $[.36,.87]$ & $.43$ $(.006)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC2 (self-ablation) & $.90$ $[.79,.97]$ & $.75$ $(.44)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC2n (C2 normed) & $.82$ $[.63,.92]$ & $.57$ $(.28)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC17 (C2 + logit gap) & $.87$ $[.66,.97]$ & $.70$ $(.44)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\emph{Live candidates that fail $\\geq$1 rule}} \\\\\nC1 (harm gain) & $.77$ $[.60,.90]$ & $.49$ $(.24)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC1n (C1 $-$ random) & $.76$ $[.52,.89]$ & $.52$ $(.24)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC6 (logit attrib.) & $.58$ $[.33,.83]$ & $.39$ $(-.06)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC2ts (two-stage) & $.70$ $[.22,.93]$ & $.46$ $(.19)$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC11 (commitment) & $.51$ $[.13,.87]$ & $.40$ $(-.27)$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC15 (eff.\\ rank) & $.45$ $[.13,.82]$ & $.13$ $(-.35)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC10 (decod.\\ area) & $-.11$ $[-.40,.24]$ & $.11$ $(-.25)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nC13 (pres.\\ invar.) & $-.08$ $[-.61,.44]$ & $.31$ $(-.42)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\emph{Dead (from prior iteration, not re-screened)}} \\\\\nC3 (twin patching) & $-.20$ & $-.20$ $(-.46)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{---} \\\\\nC7 (attn.\\ twin-diff) & $.05$ & $.10$ $(-.30)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{---} \\\\\nC8 (grad.\\ share) & $.08$ & $-.36$ $(-.54)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{---} \\\\\nC9 (template site) & $.34$ & $.38$ $(-.17)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{---} \\\\\nC16 (steering-dose slope)$^\\dagger$ & $.14$ $[-.28,.60]$ & $.45$ $(-.04)$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & $\\times$ & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{---} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\emph{Bars (never eligible to survive)}} \\\\\nLogit gap & $.77$ $[.44,.90]$ & $.76$ $(.52)^*$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nRefusal mass & $.72$ $[.47,.97]$ & $.29$ $(-.31)^*$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nBehaviour (judged) & $.93$ $[.75,.97]$ & $.82$ $(.60)^*$ & $\\checkmark$ & --- & --- & $\\checkmark$ & $\\times$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nKeyword probe & $.73$ $[.49,.95]$ & $.40$ $(.04)^*$ & $\\checkmark$ & --- & --- & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nCard regex & $.69$ $[.47,.84]$ & $.62$ $(.17)^*$ & $\\checkmark$ & --- & --- & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\emph{Not executed}} \\\\\nC4, C5 & \\multicolumn{7}{c}{NOT RUN (direction-conditioned weight paths)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$Bar partial $\\rho$ controls for $\\log_{10}$ size only. $^\\dagger$C16 is a comparator (replication of Li et al.\\ \\cite{Li2026}), not a pre-registered survivor candidate, and is barred from passing by the same rule as the bars above; it is listed here because it is one of the 14 executed candidates.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{C2 versus the logit gap.} C2 ($\\rho = 0.90$) exceeds the logit gap ($\\rho = 0.77$) by a paired lineage-bootstrap difference of $0.128$ $[0.012, 0.410]$, which excludes zero. C2 is significant within all three large families (Qwen3: $\\rho = 0.95$, $p = 0.001$; Qwen2.5: $\\rho = 1.00$, $p = 0.008$; TinyLlama: $\\rho = 1.00$, $p = 0.008$), while the logit gap reaches significance in only two (TinyLlama: $\\rho = 0.00$, $p = 0.525$). The logit gap also fails S3: the never-refuse wrapper raises it above the plain value on several models.\n\n[FIGURE:fig_screen_scatter]\n\n\\paragraph{C14 metamodel.} A 17-feature ridge metamodel under leave-one-family-out yields $\\rho = 0.75$ $[0.45, 0.93]$ with $R^2 = 0.35$. A 500-permutation identity-only control places the observed $\\rho$ above the null 95th percentile ($p = 0.002$; null 95th = 0.30). However, C14 fails S1: its partial $\\rho$ given the logit gap and size is $0.13$ (bound $-0.02$), meaning it adds no significant information beyond those baselines. The top-weighted features are C1 (0.025), C2 (0.025), h\\_auroc (0.022) and C11 (0.019).\n\n\\subsection{Detection Versus Grading}\\label{sec:detect_grade}\n\nC2's cross-family correlation may partly reflect a near-binary split. At the production depth (50\\%), only 10 of 23 graded models have $|C2| > 0.1$. The models exceeding their own random-direction null at the 95th percentile cluster in the safer half of the panel: 7 of 11 models above median $S_2$ exceed the null, versus 0 of 12 below it. The point-biserial correlation between exceeding the null and $S_2$ yields AUROC $= 0.96$, meaning C2 \\emph{detects} which models have an ablatable harm direction with near-perfect accuracy. Within the 7 models that exceed the null, restricted Spearman $\\rho = 0.64$, showing that C2 also grades severity among the models with a detectable direction.\n\nC12 shows a different pattern: 12 of 23 models exceed the null, more evenly distributed across the safety range (point-biserial $= 0.29$). C12's restricted $\\rho$ within exceeders is $0.66$.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth dependence of C2.} An exploratory sweep of C2 across 9 depth fractions (Table~\\ref{tab:depth}) shows that the correlation peaks at the pre-registered 50\\% band ($\\rho = 0.90$), with the 55\\% band close behind ($\\rho = 0.83$). The curve is not sharply peaked: fractions at 75\\% and 85\\% both reach $\\rho \\geq 0.69$. Within-family correlations peak at the same depth in all three families (Qwen3: $0.95$; Qwen2.5: $1.00$; TinyLlama: $1.00$ at 50\\%; $\\leq 0.80$ at all other fractions for TinyLlama).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{C2 Spearman $\\rho$ with $S_2$ at 9 depth fractions ($n = 23$, exploratory, post-hoc). The pre-registered production band is 50\\%.}\n\\label{tab:depth}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccccccc}\n\\toprule\nDepth & .15 & .25 & .35 & .45 & \\textbf{.50} & .55 & .65 & .75 & .85 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\rho$ ($S_2$) & .32 & .54 & .33 & .62 & \\textbf{.90} & .83 & .69 & .69 & .77 \\\\\n$n_{|C2|>0.1}$ & 4 & 4 & 3 & 6 & 10 & 11 & 14 & 13 & 12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{AMS Incumbent Comparison}\\label{sec:ams}\n\nWe implement AMS Tier-1 \\cite{Messenger2026} and run five variants on the same 23 graded checkpoints: the published formula (AMS\\_published), a mean over 3 layers (AMS\\_mean3), cross-fitted by layer (AMS\\_cf\\_layer), cross-fitted sweep (AMS\\_cf\\_sweep) and restricted to our 16 SCREEN16 prompts (AMS\\_screen16). Table~\\ref{tab:ams} reports paired rho-differences against C2 and the logit gap. Every variant loses to C2, with confidence intervals excluding zero. Four of five also lose to the logit gap.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Paired lineage-bootstrap $\\rho$-differences on the balanced target ($n = 23$). Negative = AMS variant is worse. All AMS variants fail S5 (96 prompts > 16 limit).}\n\\label{tab:ams}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nAMS variant & $\\rho$ & vs.\\ C2 (CI) & vs.\\ logit gap (CI) \\\\\n\\midrule\nAMS\\_published & .28 & $-0.62$ $[-1.01, -0.25]$ & $-0.49$ $[-0.92, -0.08]$ \\\\\nAMS\\_mean3 & .50 & $-0.40$ $[-0.68, -0.07]$ & $-0.28$ $[-0.54, +0.01]$ \\\\\nAMS\\_cf\\_layer & .33 & $-0.57$ $[-0.83, -0.17]$ & $-0.44$ $[-0.69, -0.08]$ \\\\\nAMS\\_cf\\_sweep & .21 & $-0.69$ $[-1.05, -0.33]$ & $-0.56$ $[-0.93, -0.16]$ \\\\\nAMS\\_screen16 & .48 & $-0.42$ $[-0.66, -0.14]$ & $-0.29$ $[-0.50, -0.01]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Padding bug.} The reference implementation ams-scanner 0.1.3 reads \\texttt{hidden\\_states[:,-1,:]} on right-padded batches, extracting PAD-token activations rather than the last real token. On Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, this changes the AMS sigma from 5.33 (correct, batch-size-1) to 0.74 (the package default), flipping the verdict from PASS to CRITICAL. Across the 35 models measured, our implementation produces 27 PASS / 8 WARNING / 0 CRITICAL, versus the package's 16 / 11 / 8.\n\n\\subsection{Content-Free Pull Control}\\label{sec:malla}\n\nMalla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026} showed that random steering vectors pull small models toward refusal, raising the concern that C1's signal reflects susceptibility to perturbation rather than safety content. We construct C1n by subtracting the median gain from 200 norm-matched random directions at each of three perturbation scales ($\\epsilon \\in \\{0.02, 0.05, 0.10\\}$). The central-difference form C1n\\_cd, which cancels even-order (direction-independent) perturbation effects, correlates at $\\rho = 0.76$ $[0.52, 0.89]$ with $S_2$. The correlation between C1 and C1n\\_cd across 35 models is $0.995$: the random-steer subtraction changes almost nothing.\n\nDecomposing C1 into even (direction-independent) and odd (direction-dependent) components reveals that the odd component (C1n\\_cd) carries the safety signal ($\\rho = +0.76$), while the even component (C1n\\_os, the one-sided estimate that retains the content-free pull) correlates at $\\rho = -0.45$ $[-0.72, -0.12]$. The even component is a confidently wrong-signed metric: a na\\\"ive one-sided steer would conclude that less-safe models are safer.\n\n\\subsection{Anchor Analysis: Qwen3-4B Quartet}\\label{sec:anchor}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:anchor} shows the Qwen3-4B lineage, which includes a SafeRL variant, the standard instruct model, a community-edited ``heretic'' and an abliterated version. SafeRL is the safer checkpoint by $S_2$ (0.93 vs.\\ 0.91 for Instruct), yet C2 ranks it lower (0.75 vs.\\ 1.23): a boundary case for C2, not a win. A plausible explanation is that SafeRL was trained by reinforcement learning rather than direction-level safety editing (its reward signal derives from Qwen3Guard \\cite{Zhao2025b}), which may leave a smaller trace in an ablation-sensitivity read even though it yields a safer model. The logit gap reverses the C2 ordering (SafeRL 5.0 vs.\\ Instruct 15.7), reflecting its sensitivity to output-distribution properties rather than internal geometry. The cross-fitted harm AUROC reaches 0.95 on Qwen3-4B (permutation $p = 0.025$), confirming that the harm direction is recoverable from 16 prompts at this model size. Harmful and safe activations project onto shared subspaces but with different directional emphases (first principal angle $31.1^\\circ$, max $|\\cos| = 0.20$ against a random-direction null 95th percentile of 0.42 and a positive-control value of 0.88), consistent with the ``shared subspace, different directions'' finding of Zhao et al.\\ \\cite{Zhao2025}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Qwen3-4B lineage. $S_2$: balanced safety. C2: self-ablation at 50\\% depth. LG: logit gap. {"type": "text", "file": {"filePath": "/root/.claude/projects/-ai-inventor-aii-data-runs-run-fcYd-7ruOwtm-3-invention-loop-iter-5-upd-hypo-upd-hypo/816f3b3f-fec6-43aa-8b1c-314a6f847ab9/tool-results/bvfyr5cnm.txt", "content": "\\title{One Candidate Survives: A Pre-Registered Screen of Internal Safety Metrics for Language Model Checkpoints}\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nOpen-weight language models ship faster than anyone can audit them. A metric computable from a single checkpoint's weights or activations, without generating text or consulting an external judge, could cut screening costs by orders of magnitude. We pre-register a broad panel of such candidates and evaluate most of them across several chat model families against two-sided ground truth that penalises both harmful compliance and false refusal. Under six pre-registered selection rules frozen in advance, exactly one candidate passes: twin d-prime (C12), a cross-fitted measure of how well a model's mid-layer activations separate harmful from safe prompts, with Spearman $\\rho = 0.61$ against balanced safety. The strongest raw correlation instead belongs to self-ablation sensitivity (C2, $\\rho = 0.90$), but its surviving variants turn out to be monotone transforms of one underlying measurement, and neither metric adds significant predictive power beyond simply generating and judging a small set of completions. We also compare against the published AMS incumbent, which loses to both C2 and a simple first-token logit gap on this panel, and we document a right-padding bug in its reference implementation.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nPublic model registries now host hundreds of thousands of open-weight language model checkpoints. Many are derivatives of a few base models, produced by safety fine-tuning, preference optimisation, abliteration or direct weight editing. A registry operator or downstream deployer faces a screening problem: which checkpoints are safe to serve, and which have had their safety training degraded or removed?\n\nA full behavioural evaluation requires generating completions to a prompt suite and scoring them with a rubric. This scales linearly with the number of checkpoints. If a metric computable from the model's own weights or hidden states, on at most a handful of prompts, could rank checkpoints by safety, it would cut this cost by orders of magnitude. We call such a quantity a \\emph{cheap safety metric}: one computable from a single checkpoint without its parent model, using at most 16 forward passes and no text generation.\n\nPrior work has shown that safety-trained and abliterated models differ in their activation geometry. Refusal is mediated by a low-rank subspace \\cite{Arditi2024}, harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately \\cite{Zhao2025}, and linear probes can detect harmful intent in residual streams \\cite{LlorenteSaguer2026a, LlorenteSaguer2026b}. Several reference-free safety scores have been proposed, including AMS \\cite{Messenger2026}, RAS \\cite{Huang2026}, N-GLARE \\cite{Lin2025} and Skin-Deep \\cite{Lee2026}. However, these methods evaluate on one-sided ground truth: they measure whether a model refuses harmful prompts but ignore whether it complies with benign ones. A blanket refuser scores perfectly on harm-refusal rate but is useless.\n\nThe missing ingredient is \\emph{two-sided} ground truth. We define a balanced safety score $S_2 = \\tfrac{1}{2}(\\text{harm-refusal rate}) + \\tfrac{1}{2}(\\text{benign-compliance rate})$ on a 94-item set that includes both harmful prompts and safe look-alikes (Section~\\ref{sec:d2}). Under $S_2$, a blanket refuser scores 0.5, not 1.0. This makes the screening problem harder and more realistic: a metric must track genuine safety, not mere refusal frequency.\n\nWe organise the investigation as a pre-registered screen with six selection rules, frozen before any scoring. The screen covers 14 of the 16 registered candidates plus a comparator and the published AMS incumbent, evaluated on 23 graded checkpoints from 8 families. One candidate clears all six rules. Several others, including the highest-correlation read, fail the rules designed to distinguish genuine safety signal from artefacts of the measurement.\n\n[FIGURE:fig_overview]\n\n\\paragraph{Summary of contributions.}\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{The screen.} We pre-register 16 candidate metrics and execute 14 of them (C4 and C5 remain untested) on 23 graded checkpoints from 8 architecture families against balanced two-sided safety. Under the six pre-registered rules, one candidate passes: twin d-prime (C12, $\\rho = 0.61$ $[0.36, 0.87]$). Self-ablation sensitivity (C2, $\\rho = 0.90$) clears the repaired direction-specificity and pole-control gates, but its three surviving variants (C2, C2n, C17) are monotone transforms of the same measurement (Section~\\ref{sec:screen}).\n\\item \\textbf{Two-sided ground truth on CORE-94.} We score 23 checkpoints on 94 items (66 harmful, 28 XSTest-safe) with 64-token greedy decoding and a Gemini-2.5-Flash stance judge, constructing balanced and product two-sided targets. A secondary judge (GPT-5-mini) agrees at $\\kappa = 0.72$ on a pooled 25\\% seed (Section~\\ref{sec:d2}).\n\\item \\textbf{AMS incumbent comparison.} Every variant of the published AMS metric \\cite{Messenger2026} correlates worse than C2 with two-sided safety on this panel, with paired confidence intervals excluding zero. The reference implementation (ams-scanner 0.1.3) contains a right-padding bug that reads PAD-token activations (Section~\\ref{sec:ams}).\n\\item \\textbf{Content-free pull control.} Following Malla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026}, we subtract a norm-matched random steer from C1. The corrected metric (C1n) correlates at $\\rho = 0.76$, essentially unchanged from C1 ($\\rho(C1, C1n) = 0.995$), refuting the hypothesis that C1's signal is a content-free pull toward refusal (Section~\\ref{sec:malla}).\n\\item \\textbf{Grader-side refusal.} LLM judges under the StrongREJECT rubric \\cite{Souly2024} assign the degenerate score tuple $(1,1,1)$ to 65.9\\% of harmful items, with 328 vs.\\ 67 discordant items against a stance-based framing (Section~\\ref{sec:grader}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Activation geometry of safety.}\nArditi et al.\\ \\cite{Arditi2024} showed that refusal in chat models is mediated by a single direction in the residual stream. Zhao et al.\\ \\cite{Zhao2025} demonstrated that harmfulness and refusal occupy separate subspaces. Llorente-Saguer \\cite{LlorenteSaguer2026a, LlorenteSaguer2026b} fit harm-direction probes via angular deviation, and Lee et al.\\ \\cite{Lee2026} diagnosed alignment fragility through activation geometry. Joad et al.\\ \\cite{Joad2026} argued that refusal involves more than one direction, consistent with our finding that the self-ablation signal is concentrated in the safer half of the panel (Section~\\ref{sec:detect_grade}). These papers establish that safety leaves a geometric trace in activations but do not ask whether that trace predicts a two-sided safety score across diverse families.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-free safety scoring.}\nMessenger \\cite{Messenger2026} (AMS) reports Pearson $r = -0.55$ ($p = .04$, $n = 14$) between an activation-space statistic and JailbreakBench compliance, with 96 forward passes per model. Huang et al.\\ \\cite{Huang2026} (RAS) compute a refusal-alignment score requiring a family-specific reference model. Lin et al.\\ \\cite{Lin2025} (N-GLARE, ACL 2026) build a non-generative evaluator using 7{,}000+ probing cases. Li et al.\\ \\cite{Li2026} correlate steering-dose slopes with safety, and Leong et al.\\ \\cite{Leong2025} attribute safety to template-processing sites. Hurtado \\cite{Hurtado2026} audits abliteration across families, reporting AUROC 0.95 and leave-one-family-out balanced accuracy 0.89 on a detection task. None of these methods test against two-sided ground truth that penalises false refusal.\n\n\\paragraph{Content-free steering artefacts.}\nMalla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026} showed that random steering vectors pull small models toward refusal independently of the vector's semantic content, questioning whether activation-based safety reads measure safety or merely detect a model's susceptibility to perturbation. We test this directly by subtracting a norm-matched random steer from C1 (Section~\\ref{sec:malla}).\n\n\\paragraph{Safety evaluation methodology.}\nStrongREJECT \\cite{Souly2024} provides a rubric-based judge for jailbreak evaluation. XSTest \\cite{Rottger2023} pairs harmful prompts with safe look-alikes. OR-Bench \\cite{Cui2024} measures single-turn over-refusal. GuidedBench \\cite{Huang2025} and Mu et al.\\ \\cite{Mu2026} address judge-side refusal and evaluator agreement. Qi et al.\\ \\cite{Qi2024} showed that safety alignment can be shallow, affecting only the first few tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight editing and gameability.}\nROSI \\cite{AbuShairah2025} amplifies refusal by injecting a rank-one matrix into the o\\_proj and down\\_proj residual-write matrices. Tamirisa et al.\\ \\cite{Tamirisa2024} developed tamper-resistant safeguards. Muhamed et al.\\ \\cite{Muhamed2026} introduced decoy-direction optimisation, a post-hoc defence against abliteration that could also be used to game weight-based safety reads such as C2 and C5. Item-response-theory approaches to efficient safety scoring have been proposed by Rivera et al.\\ \\cite{Rivera2026} and Spagliardi et al.\\ \\cite{Spagliardi2026}.\n\n\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Two-Sided Ground Truth}\\label{sec:d2}\n\nEach checkpoint is scored on the CORE-94 item set: 66 harmful-side items (44 from JailbreakBench and StrongREJECT, 22 XSTest contrast items) and 28 XSTest-safe items (benign prompts phrased to resemble harmful ones). Completions are generated with greedy decoding at 64 new tokens and judged by Gemini-2.5-Flash using a stance-based framing that asks whether the model engaged with or declined the request. A secondary judge (GPT-5-mini) scores a pooled 25\\% random seed across the panel, achieving inter-judge $\\kappa = 0.72$ (agreement 86.3\\%, $n = 525$ items).\n\nThe \\emph{balanced} two-sided safety score is:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:s2}\nS_2 = \\tfrac{1}{2}\\,(\\text{harm-refusal rate on 66 harmful items}) + \\tfrac{1}{2}\\,(1 - \\text{false-refusal rate on 28 XSTest-safe items}).\n\\end{equation}\nA blanket refuser scores $S_2 = 0.5$. The \\emph{product} variant $P_2 = (\\text{harm-refusal}) \\times (1 - \\text{false-refusal})$ scores a blanket refuser at~0. Both are reported; $S_2$ is the primary target throughout.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-experiment settings. All experiments use the same 23 graded checkpoints. CORE-94 = 66 harmful + 28 XSTest-safe items. SCREEN16 = 8 harmful/benign-twin pairs (16 prompts). The ROSI side arm uses its own generation length.}\n\\label{tab:settings}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nExperiment & Ground truth & Gen.\\ tokens & Judge & Prompts & Device \\\\\n\\midrule\nPanel labels ($S_2$) & CORE-94 & 64 & Gemini-2.5-Flash & 94 & --- \\\\\nMetric screen & --- & --- & --- & 16 (SCREEN16) & GPU (bf16) \\\\\nAMS incumbent & --- & --- & --- & 16 / 96 & GPU (bf16) \\\\\nROSI side arm & 64 harm + 32 twin & 20 / 128 & GPT-5-mini / Gemini & 96 & GPU \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Panel}\\label{sec:panel}\n\nWe measure 36 checkpoints from 8 architecture families (Qwen3, Qwen2.5, TinyLlama, Llama-3.2, OLMo-2, SmolLM2, Falcon3, Danube3) spanning 11 lineages. All are at most 4B parameters and load in bf16. Of the 36, 23 are graded (have two-sided labels), 12 are ungraded (metric values computed; labels deferred), and 1 is a base model without chat formatting. Two graded checkpoints are blanket refusers (Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B CensorTune variants). Eleven checkpoints are honest instruct models (from families with $\\geq$3 graded members), used in the pole-control rule. Twelve blind Set~A checkpoints were frozen before any Set~A label existed; Phi-3.5-mini-instruct was skipped (exceeded the 10.5\\,GB VRAM budget), leaving 11 frozen blind.\n\nAt the panel's realised size ($n = 23$; intraclass correlation ICC $= 0.103$ across 11 lineages, i.e.\\ sibling checkpoints from the same base model are only slightly more alike than unrelated ones), the minimum detectable effect at 80\\% power is $|\\rho| = 0.53$. Null results at this panel size mean ``not detectable,'' not ``absent.''\n\n\\subsection{Candidate Metrics}\\label{sec:candidates}\n\nWe pre-register 16 candidates grouped into seven conceptual families (PREREG SHA-256: \\texttt{9da2b165}), each reading hidden states or weights of a single model on at most 16 prompts (the frozen SCREEN16 set, 8 harmful/benign-twin pairs drawn from XSTest and JailbreakBench):\n\n\\paragraph{I. Causal gain (C1--C3).} C1: finite-difference harm-to-refusal gain (perturb the residual stream by the harm direction at 50\\% depth, measure the refusal-probability change). C2: two-sided self-ablation sensitivity (ablate the harm direction, measure the drop in harmful-vs-benign separation relative to an anisotropy-matched random direction). C3: twin-patching flip depth (patch activations from a harmful twin into its benign twin, measure at which layer the output flips).\n\n\\paragraph{II. Direction-conditioned weight paths (C4--C5).} C4: writer capacity along the refusal axis. C5: MLP-path gain from harm to refusal directions. Both remain untested.\n\n\\paragraph{III. Component and routing (C6--C9).} C6: logit-attribution concentration (cross-fitted, in-sample and lens variants measured). C7: attention mass on twin-differing tokens. C8: gradient$\\times$input share on twin-differing tokens. C9: template-site share \\cite{Leong2025}.\n\n\\paragraph{IV. Depth and site (C10--C11).} C10: decodability-minus-drive area. C11: first 8-token response-window commitment trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{V. Two-sided geometry (C12--C13).} C12: twin d-prime along a cross-fitted harm axis at the middle layer (cross-fitted: the harm direction is estimated on one half of the 16 prompts and applied to the other). C13: presentation invariance across prompt rephrasings.\n\n\\paragraph{VI. Learned (C14).} A ridge metamodel combining all available features under leave-one-family-out cross-validation, with a 500-permutation identity-only control.\n\n\\paragraph{VII. Label-free (C15--C16).} C15: late effective rank (spectral dispersion at final layers). C16: steering-dose slope (comparator only, replication of Li et al.\\ \\cite{Li2026}).\n\nThe logit-only baselines enter as bars, never as survivors: the first-token logit gap (the difference between the model's logit for a refusal-indicating first token and a compliance-indicating first token) and refusal-token mass (the softmax probability mass on refusal tokens at that position). C4 and C5 were not executed and are listed as NOT RUN. We also tested C1n (C1 minus a norm-matched random steer, following Malla et al.\\ \\cite{Malla2026}), C2n (C2 standardised by its own direction null), C2ts (a two-stage variant of C2), and C17 (the mean reference-rank of C2 and the logit gap).\n\n\\subsection{Selection Rules}\\label{sec:rules}\n\nA candidate passes the screen if it satisfies all six pre-registered rules (hashed before any scoring):\n\n\\begin{description}\n\\item[S1 (incremental signal).] The one-sided 95\\% lineage-cluster bootstrap CI on the partial Spearman $\\rho$ with $S_2$, after removing the logit gap and $\\log_{10}$ parameter count, excludes zero.\n\\item[S2 (direction specificity).] (a) The within-lineage label-permutation $p < 0.05$, and (b) the metric exceeds its anisotropy-matched random-direction null at the 95th percentile on a majority of the graded panel.\n\\item[S3 (pole control).] The declared orientation holds, and both wrapped poles (always-refuse and never-refuse system prompts) plus blanket refusers score worse than the honest instruct checkpoint from the same family.\n\\item[S4 (sign agreement).] Same sign at checkpoint and family aggregation.\n\\item[S5 (efficiency).] At most 16 prompts and under 2 minutes per $\\sim$4B model.\n\\item[S6 (beyond confounds).] Beats the family-only and size-only out-of-fold predictors.\n\\end{description}\n\nS2(b) and S3 were repaired before the iteration-5 evaluation: the original S2(b) required $\\geq$60\\% of models to exceed the random-direction null, a threshold that only the in-sample AMS variant met (at 100\\%, consistent with overfitting). The repaired form requires a simple majority. The original S3 required all $2 \\times 11$ pole checks to hold; the repair requires all checks that are evaluable (excluding models whose candidate value is near zero, $|C| < 0.01$, where $C$ is the candidate's raw score and the pole comparison is noise at that scale). Both original and repaired verdicts are reported.\n\n\\section{Experimental Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nAll measurements run on a single NVIDIA L4 GPU (24\\,GB) in bf16 precision. VRAM peaks at 9.7\\,GB for 4B models. Per-model wall time ranges from 13 to 107 seconds (median 31). The 23 graded checkpoints span 360M to 4B parameters. The 16-prompt SCREEN16 set, the selection rules and the candidate list were frozen (SHA-256 hashed) before any metric value was computed. All 1{,}195 stance-judge lookups hit cache (\\$0.00 incremental cost). Set~A values were frozen (SHA-256: \\texttt{95d97ac3}) at 2026-09-21T23:21:43Z, before any Set~A label existed. Code, checkpoint values and null distributions are released.\n\n\\section{Results}\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{The Screen}\\label{sec:screen}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:screen} reports the selection-rule verdicts for all executed candidates. One candidate passes all six rules: C12 (twin d-prime), with $\\rho = 0.61$ $[0.36, 0.87]$ under the balanced target and partial $\\rho = 0.43$ (one-sided 5\\% lineage-bootstrap bound $0.006$, clearing S1 by a narrow margin). C12 is significant within Qwen3 ($\\rho = 0.67$, $n = 8$) and Qwen2.5 ($\\rho = 0.70$, $n = 5$) but not TinyLlama ($\\rho = -0.10$, $n = 5$).\n\n[FIGURE:fig_c12_scatter]\n\nUnder the repaired S2(b) and S3 rules, three additional candidates survive: C2 ($\\rho = 0.90$), C2n ($\\rho = 0.82$) and C17 ($\\rho = 0.87$). However, these three are \\emph{not independent measurements}: C2n is C2 standardised by its own direction null, and C17 is the mean reference-rank of C2 and the logit gap. All three are monotone functions of the same self-ablation measurement plus, for C17, the logit-gap bar. Shipping three survivors satisfies the letter of a ``three independent internal metrics'' requirement but not its spirit.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pre-registered screen on the balanced target ($n = 23$ graded checkpoints, 8 families, 11 lineages). $\\rho$: Spearman with $S_2$.
